| **[Zoom Where the Answer Looks: Cross-Resolution Self-Distillation for MLLMs under Visual-Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32664v2)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Under the small per-view visual-token budgets of efficient deployment, the fine text of documents, charts and infographics becomes illegible to multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Zooming in recovers it, but the region must be chosen before the model answers. Existing methods decode an answer or a box first, at the cost of generation, or read the attention available before answering, which localizes the evidence only in top layers whose depth varies with model size. Learned region proposers instead distill the attention of the model's answers, but on the same budgeted view. Yet this attention is precise only on a view the model can read: on Qwen3-VL-4B, a window around its peak covers 83% of the evidence on a high-resolution view but 54% on the budgeted view. Since answers and high-resolution views are available during training, we introduce LookZoom, a zooming method that trains a dedicated look token by cross-resolution self-distillation of the answer's attention: with the MLLM frozen, the token learns to attend on the budgeted view, before any answer, where the model's own correct answers attend on a high-resolution view. The region around its attention peak is the window to zoom into, and reinforcement learning then trains the MLLM to answer from both views. With 98 visual tokens per view on five text-rich benchmarks, LookZoom surpasses attention-guided zooming by 20-24 points and grounding-based zooming and learned crop routing by 12-15 points on Qwen3-VL at three scales, and it outperforms learned region proposers at every budget from 98 to 392 tokens. On Qwen3-VL, the look token alone, with the MLLM frozen, already surpasses every baseline that leaves the language model unchanged, and the recipe carries over to InternVL3.5 and Qwen2.5-VL.</p></details> |  |
| **[BusterX++: Towards Unified Cross-Modal AI-Generated Content Detection and Explanation with MLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14632v5)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid advancement of generative AI has substantially improved image and video synthesis, amplifying the risk of multimodal visual misinformation. Recent MLLMs have shown promise for transparent AI-generated content detection through reasoning and explanation, yet existing approaches largely treat image and video forensics as isolated tasks, leaving cross-modal synergies underexplored. To address this, we present \textbf{BusterX++}, a unified MLLM for joint image and video detection with interpretable reasoning. We also introduce \textbf{GenBuster-Bench++}, a curated benchmark containing balanced image and video samples spanning recent generation models and diverse real-world scenarios, with synthetic images and videos screened using the same human difficulty criterion. Using this controlled setting, we revisit the widely adopted SFT+RL post-training paradigm. Notably, in the unified image-video setting, our results demonstrate that a single-stage pure RL strategy driven primarily by sparse outcome rewards outperforms a strong SFT+RL baseline. We observe that single-stage pure RL maintains higher policy entropy than SFT-initialized RL throughout training. This observation suggests a possible role for exploration in the performance gains observed under unified image-video training. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BusterX++ achieves state-of-the-art performance, highlighting the powerful potential of RL for unified cross-modal visual reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[BusterX: MLLM-Powered AI-Generated Video Forgery Detection and Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12620v9)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As generative video models become increasingly realistic, detecting AI-generated videos requires systems that offer both accuracy and interpretability. However, applying Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to video forensics is currently limited by outdated datasets, simplistic evaluation protocols, and a reliance on black-box classification. To address these issues, we introduce a comprehensive dataset, benchmark, and baseline model for video forgery detection. First, we present \textbf{GenBuster-200K}, a fair dataset of over 200,000 high-quality videos sourced from state-of-the-art generators, featuring diverse real-world scenarios. Second, we propose \textbf{GenBuster-Bench}, a diagnostic benchmark spanning three progressive tracks (In-Domain, Out-of-Domain, and In-the-Wild) to evaluate models across \textit{domain shifts} and \textit{generational shifts}. Finally, we develop \textbf{BusterX}, a simple MLLM baseline with Reinforcement Learning (RL) training. Instead of direct binary classification, BusterX formulates detection as a visual reasoning task, where the generated reasoning chain serves as detector itself. Experimental results demonstrate that BusterX outperforms several leading MLLMs in both detection accuracy and rationale quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[HumanSightBench: A Benchmark for Human Visual Perception in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12305v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on knowledge-intensive and reasoning-based vision-language benchmark datasets, but still struggle to solve the visual questions that people can solve easily. Evaluating the differences between MLLMs and human-level visual perception is challenging due to end-to-end evaluation which combines various skills, or because we are uncertain if individual items are valid. Specifically, observed errors may reflect visual perception, language priors, background knowledge, question ambiguity, and underspecified answers. Our guideline for HumanSightBench is that we construct items that are human-easy by construction and model-hard by evaluation. HumanSightBench contains 2,876 visual questions with short answers across 7 top-level perceptual categories and 21 subcategories. We only include items that pass five inclusion gates: question clarity, answer uniqueness, visual grounding, human solvability, and text-only shortcut filtering. According to the protocol, errors are defined as incorrect answers on human-validated visual tasks with the evaluated interface. The inclusion gates are intended to reduce confounds from ambiguity and external knowledge. The best pass@3 score is 65.6% across eight evaluated MLLMs. No evaluated model exceeds 27.5% on Counting. These results identify capability gaps that are hidden by overall scores and can help future training experiments and held-out evaluation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can MLLMs Reason over Surround-View Driving Videos? A Multi-View Autonomous Driving QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15225v1)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Surround-view video understanding in autonomous driving is a crucial capability for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to achieve reliable driving reasoning. Real-world driving often requires information beyond the front-view camera. Existing MLLMs primarily focus on front-view information, while their spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning abilities remain unknown. To address this gap, we propose MVDriveBench, a high-information-density video QA benchmark for autonomous driving, characterized by synchronized surround-view inputs, spatio-temporal understanding, and reasoning-chain-oriented evaluation. We design 12 categories of QA tasks that cover the complete reasoning pipeline from low-level visual perception to high-level driving decision-making, enabling a systematic evaluation of MLLMs surround-view spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning capabilities in complex driving environments. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 23 open-source, closed-source, and expert MLLMs on MVDriveBench. The results show that current MLLMs still exhibit insufficient surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning ability. Meanwhile, closed-loop evaluations of specialist models in AlpaSim reveal a correlation between surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities and closed-loop driving performance, highlighting the importance of comprehensive multi-view understanding for reliable vehicle control.</p></details> |  |
| **[Let's Think in Two Steps: Mitigating Agreement Bias in MLLMs with Self-Grounded Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11662v4)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Verifiers--functions assigning rewards to agent behavior--have been key to AI progress in math, code, and games. However, extending gains to domains without clear-cut success criteria (e.g., computer use) remains a challenge: while humans can recognize desired outcomes, translating this intuition into scalable rules is nontrivial. Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) offer a promising solution, given their world knowledge, human-preference alignment, and reasoning skills. We evaluate MLLM verifiers across web navigation, computer use, and robotics, spanning 13 models, 28 judge designs, diverse agent trajectories, and distinct downstream applications. We identify a critical limitation: a strong tendency for MLLMs to over-validate agent behavior, a phenomenon we term agreement bias. This bias is pervasive across models, is resilient to test-time scaling, and harms pipelines relying on MLLM judgments (e.g., self-improvement). We discuss principles for design and evaluation of verifiers, and introduce SGV, a method that improves verification by harnessing MLLMs' own capabilities via modulation of (un)conditional generation. First, an MLLM generates broad priors about desired behavior, independent of the data under evaluation. Then, conditioned on generated priors, it reasons over and judges a candidate trajectory. Our methods yield more human-aligned verifiers, with gains of up to 25pp in failure detection, 14pp in accuracy, and benefits extending to downstream applications. In self-improvement and online supervision, our verifiers boost task completion of a GUI specialist in OSWorld, a diffusion policy in robomimic, and a ReAct agent in VisualWebArena--surpassing the previous SOTA by 20pp. Finally, we release an updated version of VisualWebArena, featuring improved evaluators, high-fidelity environment parallelism with >10x speedups, and VWA-Lite, a 1/3-scale subset with comparable evaluation fidelity.</p></details> | <details><summary>ICLR ...</summary><p>ICLR 2026. Code, models, and data publicly available at https://mshalimay.github.io/agreement-bias-sgv/</p></details> |
| **[Robust Reasoning Segmentation for Open-World MLLMs without Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16785v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ensuring trustworthiness and versatility in Open-World Visual Recognition is a core goal of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While integrating segmentation is desirable, prevailing methods require invasive fine-tuning, which often leads to catastrophic forgetting of the model's general-purpose capabilities. This trade-off between task-specific performance and general reliability fundamentally undermines the goal of building unified, trustworthy systems. We introduce LENS (Leveraging kEypoiNts for MLLMs' Segmentation), a novel plug-and-play paradigm designed to equip MLLMs with pixel-level ability without compromising their foundational integrity. LENS attaches a lightweight head to a completely frozen MLLM, interpreting the intrinsic spatial cues embedded in attention maps. It extracts keypoints and translates them into prompt-compatible features for a mask decoder. Extensive experiments validate that LENS achieves segmentation performance competitive with retraining-based methods. Crucially, it largely preserves the MLLM's open-world generalization, avoiding the degradation observed in fine-tuned counterparts. By decoupling segmentation from generation, LENS offers a robust and efficient path toward truly multi-talented and reliable vision models.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in International Journal of Computer Vision (IJCV), 2026</p></details> |
| **[TAMEing the Open-World Personalization: Towards Open-Set Personalized MLLM Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14118v1)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personalizing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) as intelligent assistants has recently emerged as an active research topic, where an MLLM is expected to go beyond generic, one-size-fits-all replies, and instead generate responses grounded in user-specific objects, entities, and concepts encountered during interactions. Nevertheless, existing studies mainly focus on personalizing MLLMs on a static, predefined set of personalized concepts and require users to manually and frequently extend this set to accommodate newly emerging concepts, which is infeasible in dynamic-world scenarios. To address this limitation, we recast conventional MLLM personalization as an Open-Set problem, and propose TAME-O, the first long-context open-set personalized MLLM assistant. Specifically, TAME-O introduces a training-free, plug-and-play Concept Reconciliation Skill (CR Skill). It enables the assistant to automatically unlock novel concepts during prolonged human-MLLM interactions while preventing confusion with previously unlocked ones, allowing the MLLM to continually grasp more and more concepts and progressively refine the modeling of known ones. Extensive experiments under the open-set personalization setting underscore the efficacy of our method.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 Main Track; code and data are available at https://github.com/ronpay/TAME-O</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Proxy Metrics: MLLMs as Human Surrogates for Explainable AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13926v1)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Explainable AI (XAI) has expanded into a broad and impactful research area, but the fundamental question of how best to evaluate explanation techniques has proven frustratingly elusive. Methodologies typically revolve around either proxy metrics which tell us little about the utility of an explanation, or human evaluation which---while generally considered the gold standard---is expensive, difficult to scale, and challenging to reproduce. In this paper, we investigate if Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can act as surrogate human participants to automate the evaluation of XAI. To do this, we first propose a ``purpose-grounded'' evaluation framework with metrics applicable to both MLLMs and human users alike. Then, we offer some conceptual insights as to why and when we can trust these models to automate this process. Lastly, we conduct extensive empirical testing across five purpose-grounded areas such as model improvement and fourteen popular MLLMs. Results indicate that all models trend positively, but OpenAI and Qwen models generally show higher alignment compared to other variants, which would be the recommended choices in practice. We expect our insights not to replace user testing, but rather to help XAI researchers complement their current evaluation methodologies and move the field away from heavy reliance on proxy metrics for measuring explanation utility.</p></details> | <details><summary>Neura...</summary><p>Neural Information Processing Systems. NeurIPS 2026. Sydney</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Modality Preference: Controlling Multimodal Arbitration under Modality Conflict in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12883v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) integrate information across modalities, but become unreliable when those modalities disagree. Under conflicting inputs, MLLMs may favor modalities that are not the most reliable for the task, thus motivating inference-time control of modality arbitration. We identify a small set of attention heads, termed arbitration heads, that causally influence whether predictions follow visual or textual evidence. Building on this, we introduce a lightweight, training-free intervention that biases these heads toward a designated trusted modality at inference time. The identified heads are stable across calibration set sizes and generalize across settings, and steering them enables effective bidirectional control of modality reliance across diverse benchmarks. Our findings identify an effective control mechanism to align MLLM predictions with task-specified modality priorities. The code is available at https://github.com/zhaoyingpan/modality-arbitration.</p></details> | WACV 2027 |
| **[Conceal, Reconstruct, Jailbreak: Exploiting the Reconstruction-Concealment Tradeoff in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05709v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Intent-obfuscation-based jailbreak attacks on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) transform a harmful query into a concealed multimodal input to bypass safety mechanisms. We study these attacks through the lens of a reconstruction-concealment tradeoff: the transformed input must sufficiently obscure harmful intent while preserving enough information for the victim model to recover the underlying request. Through a reconstruction and concealment analysis of representative black-box methods, we find that existing transformations struggle to balance this tradeoff, limiting their effectiveness. In contrast, we show that character-removed variants, constructed by randomly removing characters from the original query, achieve a better balance, motivating their use as the basis for our attack construction. Building on this observation, we propose concealment-aware variant construction, which selects character-removed variants with low harmful keyword/phrase alignment and high mutual diversity, and instantiates them through five prompting strategies that distribute the transformed information across text and image channels. We further introduce keyword/phrase-related distractor images that depict the harmful concept in diverse contexts, providing more effective distraction than generic distractor images. Experiments across diverse closed-source and open-source MLLMs show that the proposed strategies substantially outperform the evaluated baselines. Code and reproduction instructions are available at https://github.com/Farhamdur/CRJ.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 camera-ready version; 43 pages, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[Beyond RGB: Benchmarking and Enhancing MLLMs for Hyperspectral Image Understanding via Training-Free Reasoning Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08884v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on RGB image understanding, yet their ability to use spectral evidence beyond the visible range remains largely unexplored. Hyperspectral imagery (HSI) provides dense spectral measurements that reveal material and environmental cues unavailable in RGB, but current MLLMs cannot directly ingest high-dimensional HSI. To study this gap, we introduce HM-Bench, an evidence-grounded benchmark for hyperspectral image understanding with MLLMs. HM-Bench contains 19,337 question--answer pairs from 2,178 hyperspectral samples across 13 task categories, covering general perception, spectral reasoning, and spatial--spectral reasoning. To make HSI accessible to the native image--text interfaces of existing MLLMs, we further propose VSR$^{2}$, a training-free Visual--Spectral--Report Reasoning framework. VSR$^{2}$ represents each HSI sample with three aligned views: an RGB image for visual semantics and spatial context, a PCA-based image for dominant spectral variation, and a structured report for quantitative spectral--spatial evidence. Under a controlled RGB-only versus VSR$^{2}$ evaluation protocol, experiments on 17 representative MLLMs show that HSI information improves average accuracy from 38.90% to 40.95%, with gains varying substantially across models and task categories. These results indicate that HSI information (beyond RGB) is useful for current MLLMs, while robust hyperspectral reasoning remains an open challenge.</p></details> |  |
| **[BlenderFORGE: Framework for Optimizing Reactive 3D-Graphics Editing Ability of MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10768v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown promise as interactive agents, yet precise, programmatic 3D graphics editing remains difficult to train because existing systems largely rely on closed-source model APIs and lack deterministic, state-verifiable execution environments. We present BlenderFORGE, a trainable data-environment-optimization framework for open-weight MLLM agents that edit explicit Blender scenes through Blender Python (bpy) code generation. BlenderFORGE targets four fundamental state-verifiable editing task families: object placement, shape-key editing, lighting adjustment, and material modification. It integrates three components: (1) a scalable perturbation-based data generation pipeline built from curated assets and large-scale 3D scene repositories; (2) an isolated Blender Sandbox for deterministic code execution, structured state export, and closed-loop reward computation; and (3) a two-stage training pipeline that uses offline teacher trajectories for cold-start Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and sandbox-computed rewards for multi-turn tool-agentic Reinforcement Learning (AgentLoop-RL). Experiments across the four task families show that the resulting open-weight Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3.5-9B BlenderFORGE agents substantially improve over their base models and achieve competitive performance with several zero-shot proprietary MLLM baselines on selected metrics. Additional cross-benchmark evaluations and camera-reframing experiments provide evidence of transfer beyond the original training setting.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 35 pages, including references, appendix, and checklist. Project page: https://sensetime-fvg.github.io/BlenderFORGE/</p></details> |
| **[One Agent to Guide Them All: Empowering MLLMs for Vision-and-Language Navigation via Explicit World Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15400v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A navigation agent must understand high-level semantic instructions and reason about precise spatial structure. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), with their strong generalization, are a promising foundation for such agents. We propose GTA, a decoupled design that separates low-level spatial state estimation from high-level semantic planning. We introduce an explicit metric world representation that combines accumulated geometry with navigation history and exposes this information to the MLLM through a multimodal interface for decision-making. We further introduce counterfactual branch reasoning that checks anticipated outcomes against subsequent observations and revises navigation decisions when they disagree. The world representation grounds waypoint selection in reconstructed metric geometry. GTA achieves 48.8% Success Rate (SR) on the full R2R-CE val-unseen split and 46.2% on a 260-episode English RxR-CE val-unseen subset. Additional comparisons on shared R2R-CE episodes and mechanism ablations support the framework's effectiveness. We also demonstrate transfer to wheeled, quadrupedal, and aerial robots. Project page: https://zeruili22.github.io/GTA/</p></details> | CoRL 2026 |
| **[Fundus-R1: Training a Fundus-Reading MLLM on Public Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08322v3)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fundus imaging such as CFP, OCT and SLO is crucial for the early detection of retinal anomalies and diseases. Fundus image understanding, due to its knowledge-intensive nature, poses a challenging vision-language task. An emerging approach to addressing the task is to post-train a generic multimodal large language model (MLLM), either by supervised finetuning (SFT) or by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), on a considerable amount of in-house samples paired with high-quality clinical reports. However, these valuable samples are not publicly accessible, which not only hinders reproducibility but also practically limits research to few players. In this paper, we make a novel attempt to train a reasoning-enhanced fundus-reading MLLM, termed Fundus-R1, using exclusively public datasets, wherein over 94% of the data are annotated with image-level labels only. Our technical contributions are two-fold. First, we propose a RAG-based method for composing image-specific, knowledge-aware reasoning traces. Such auto-generated traces link visual findings identified by a generic MLLM to the image labels in terms of ophthalmic knowledge. Second, we enhance RLVR with a process reward that encourages self-consistency of the generated reasoning trace in each rollout. Extensive experiments on three fundus-reading benchmarks, i.e., FunBench, Omni-Fundus and GMAI-Fundus, show that Fundus-R1 clearly outperforms multiple baselines, including its generic counterpart (Qwen2.5-VL) and a stronger edition post-trained without using the generated traces. This work paves the way for training powerful fundus-reading MLLMs with publicly available data.</p></details> |  |
| **[MaRiO: Multi-agent Collaborative Reasoning via Shared Observations in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10344v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show strong visual perception capabilities, but existing evaluations largely focus on single-view or multi-view settings with shared camera parameters, leaving the challenge of multi-agent collaborative scenarios underexplored. In such settings, multiple agents observe a shared environment from independent viewpoints, and reliable decisions require reasoning across these independent observations of the same environment. To study this problem, we introduce MaRiO, a benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaborative reasoning, and evaluate 23 state-of-the-art MLLMs on it. Our analysis reveals three key findings: (1) tasks governed by static perception are generally easier than tasks that require transformational event sequences; (2) most models struggle to resolve cross-agent correspondences, with errors increasing with scene complexity; and (3) models show limited ability to leverage geometric cues present in the scene. As an initial step toward addressing these challenges, we explore geometry-guided synthetic view augmentation that generates intermediate transition frames between agent observations, providing auxiliary spatial context that improves performance in certain categories. Overall, our results highlight that multi-agent collaborative reasoning remains an open challenge and an important direction for multimodal embodied systems. The code is available on the project page: https://omarftt.github.io/MaRiO-page/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations & Datasets Track (poster). First two authors contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[Read It Back: Pretrained MLLMs Are Zero-Shot Reward Models for Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11886v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we propose SpectraReward, a training-free reward function that turns pretrained MLLMs into off-the-shelf reward models for image-generation reinforcement learning. Instead of asking the MLLM to judge a generated image or answer decomposed verification questions, SpectraReward measures how well the original prompt can be recovered from the generated image through a single image-conditioned, teacher-forced forward pass. We use the average image-conditioned prompt log-likelihood as the reward, directly reusing the MLLM's pretrained image-text alignment ability without preference labels, reward-model fine-tuning. We further introduce Self-SpectraReward, a special case for unified multimodal models where the policy's own understanding branch serves as the reward model for its generation branch, forming a closed-loop self-improving framework without external reward models or external knowledge. Extensive experiments validate SpectraReward through a broad image-generation RL study covering two diffusion models, three RL algorithms, nine reward MLLM backbones from four MLLM families spanning 4B to 235B parameters, and five out-of-distribution text-to-image benchmarks. Results show that both SpectraReward and Self-SpectraReward significantly and consistently improve generation performance and outperform prior MLLM-derived reward training methods. Further analysis reveals that larger reward MLLMs are not always better, while Self-SpectraReward can match or surpass much larger external reward models, suggesting that reward-policy alignment is a key factor for effective image-generation RL. Project Page: https://huangrh99.github.io/SpectraReward/</p></details> |  |
| **[OpenView: Empowering MLLMs with Out-of-view VQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18563v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) show great potential in natural image understanding. Yet, they perform well, mainly on reasoning in-view contents within the image frame. This paper presents the first study on out-of-view (OOV) understanding, i.e., the ability to reason about objects, activities, and scenes beyond the visible frame of a perspective view. Our technical contributions are threefold. First, we design OpenView, a four-stage pipeline that leverages panoramic imagery for large-scale and diverse multi-choice VQA synthesis, where full-scene coverage enables flexible view framing while preserving global scene awareness. Second, we curate OpenView-Dataset, a high-quality synthetic dataset from diverse real-world panoramas to empower MLLMs upon supervised fine-tuning. Third, we build OpenView-Bench, a benchmark that jointly measures choice and rationale accuracy for interpretable and diagnosable evaluation. Experimental results show that despite having a large gap from human performance in OOV VQA answer selection, multiple MLLMs, upon empowered by OpenView, can consistently boost their performance, uplifted from 48.3% to 62.3% on average. Code, benchmark, and data are available at https://github.com/q1xiangchen/OpenView.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Code: https://github.com/q1xiangchen/OpenView</p></details> |
| **[ProQuant: Progressive Quantization-aware Training for Edge MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08216v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) with impressive perception and understanding capabilities will enable a range of downstream applications. However, their substantial computational and memory requirements hinder the real-world implementation, particularly for resource-constrained edge devices. Quantization has emerged as an effective solution for deploying large models. While post-training quantization (PTQ) successfully retains the performance at moderate bit-width (e.g., W8A8), it suffers from severe performance degradation under low-bit quantization (e.g., W4A8), especially for small-scale models that are more suitable for edge deployment. In this paper, we propose ProQuant, a novel weight-activation quantization-aware training (QAT) framework tailored for low-bit MLLMs. First, to align the limited finetuning dataset distribution with the pretrained dataset distribution, we propose a self-relabeling (SRL) scheme to resample the responses of the open-source multimodal dataset. Second, we are the first to take a decoupled view of multi-modal (MM) understanding and language modeling abilities. We propose a progressive block activation (PBA) mechanism to prioritize the MM understanding recovery. We also introduce a vision-sensitive factor $\vecτ$ that effectively identifies the MM-critical layers. Extensive experiments across various benchmarks, covering models with 2B-8B parameters, show that ProQuant significantly outperforms existing methods. For example, our W4A8 Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct improves average accuracy by impressively 6.6% and achieves performance comparable to full precision counterparts with only approximately 1% performance drop. Code is available at https://github.com/yufeixue-ai/ProQuant/.</p></details> |  |
| **[PSGAgent: Enhancing MLLMs' Dynamic Visual Perception for Panoptic Scene Graph Generation via Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07408v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Panoptic Scene Graph generation (PSG) requires holistic yet fine-grained reasoning over complex relations among objects. However, current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) applied to PSG suffer from a perception bottleneck: they function as static observers limited by fixed-resolution views. As a result, models often overlook critical relations involving small or peripheral objects and struggle to differentiate ambiguous predicates in complex scenes. To address these limitations, we propose PSGAgent, an agentic framework that enhances visual perception for PSG through a think-act-observe loop. PSGAgent allows MLLMs to dynamically refine their visual perception using two tools: a Region Zoom-In Tool for local exploration of small or boundary relations and a Visual Highlight Tool for relation-focused segmentation that filters out irrelevant contextual noise. To optimize decision-making, we construct a two-stage training pipeline. First, we create a high-quality, error-aware Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (M-CoT) dataset that provides robust supervision for cold-start learning. Then, we design a novel hierarchical reward mechanism integrated with Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO) to enable strategic and adaptive tool usage. Extensive experiments on the PSG benchmark show that PSGAgent achieves SOTA performance, demonstrating strong capability in both holistic and fine-grained scene understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ACM Multimedia 2026. 10 pages, 8 figures</p></details> |
| **[Breaking Bad Molecules: Are MLLMs Ready for Structure-Level Molecular Detoxification?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10912v5)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Toxicity remains a leading cause of early-stage drug development failure. Despite advances in molecular design and property prediction, the task of molecular toxicity repair, generating structurally valid molecular alternatives with reduced toxicity, has not yet been systematically defined or benchmarked. To fill this gap, we introduce ToxiMol, the first benchmark task for general-purpose Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) focused on molecular toxicity repair. We construct a standardized dataset covering 11 primary tasks and 660 representative toxic molecules spanning diverse mechanisms and granularities. We design a prompt annotation pipeline with mechanism-aware and task-adaptive capabilities, informed by expert toxicological knowledge. In parallel, we propose an automated evaluation framework, ToxiEval, which integrates toxicity endpoint prediction, synthetic accessibility, drug-likeness, and structural similarity into a high-throughput evaluation chain for repair success. We systematically assess 43 mainstream general-purpose MLLMs and conduct multiple ablation studies to analyze key issues, including evaluation metrics, candidate diversity, and failure attribution. Experimental results show that although current MLLMs still face significant challenges on this task, they begin to demonstrate promising capabilities in toxicity understanding, semantic constraint adherence, and structure-aware editing.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by KDD 2026 (AI for Sciences Track, Oral)</p></details> |
| **[Where to Connect? Boosting MLLMs via Dynamic Gated Pathways across ALL ViT and LLM Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06844v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central yet under-explored question in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is where to connect---how visual information from a hierarchical vision encoder should be wired into the layer-wise semantics of a large language model. Recent multi-layer fusion methods either compress hierarchical features on the encoder side or rely on predefined sparse or hierarchical layer-to-layer connections, and therefore stop short of modeling adaptive cross-layer interactions between the ViT and LLM hierarchies. We address this gap with DGP (Dynamic Gated Pathways), a routing mechanism that establishes all-to-all connections between every ViT layer and every LLM layer, with each LLM layer dynamically gating its preference over visual layers conditioned on its own semantic state. Built on top of LLaVA-1.5-7B and 13B, DGP delivers consistent gains over the LLaVA-1.5-7B baseline (e.g., +4.2 on SQA$^{I}$, +3.3 on MM-Vet, +3.1 on VizWiz and LLaVA$^{W}$) and surpasses prior multi-layer visual fusion methods on most benchmarks. Accepted at the 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |

On a sketch, the sketch is the image, and text prompts detect, point, resolve object and spatial referring expressions, and ground affordances in it. TREC 2025 Workshop, Dec. 2025</p></details> |

| **[What Makes a Good Visual Representation for Spatial Understanding in MLLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17844v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial understanding is a necessary step in moving multimodal large language models (MLLMs) toward reasoning about the 3D physical world. Their visual pretraining data, however, is almost entirely 2D, so the visual tokens processed by MLLMs often lack inherent 3D structural information. A growing number of methods inject such structure by transferring representations from external vision or geometry models, and collectively establish that such transfer helps. Behind these methods lies a largely underexplored question: what makes a visual representation good for spatial understanding? To answer it, we first separate what is transferred from how it is transferred, and then trace the resulting differences back to properties of the representations themselves. What we transfer comes from 3D geometry models, self-supervised image and video models, vision-language models, and generative models, in the form of latent features or explicit depth and point maps. We transfer it either by distillation, using it as a supervision target, or by fusion, feeding it as an additional input. We find that a representation's 3D structure correlates strongly with how much it helps, measured by cross-view consistency for latent features and by depth and reconstruction accuracy for explicit geometry. We further show that the form of a representation dictates the mechanism it needs. Latent features gain as much from fusion as from distillation; explicit geometry gains little from fusion, and most of its distillation gain depends on a geometry decoding head. Guided by these findings, we distill both latent features and explicit geometry into a single MLLM. | **[When, Where, and Why: Hierarchical Causal Explainability for Video Face Understanding with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17791v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding facial behaviour in video requires answering three fundamental questions: when a key facial expression occurs, where the model attends on the face at that moment, and why that evidence is necessary rather than merely correlated. First, we introduce ExplainFace, a weakly supervised video-text corpus for facial behaviour understanding, automatically constructed from CelebV-HQ videos and validated through human evaluation and a two-model audit (at most 2.3% contradictions), demonstrating consistent gains across two backbone architectures. Second, H-TCC (Hierarchical Temporal Spatial Causal Concepts), which finds the key temporal moments (When), the facial regions (Where), and verifies its causal necessity (Why) through targeted occlusion. Fine-tuning on ExplainFace raises appearance Macro F1 from 19.48% to 32.87% (+13.39), with gains of +4.38 on actions, +6.77 on temporal emotion, and +11.84 F1 on deepfake detection. | **[VITAL: Visual-Semantic Dual Supervision for Enhanced and Interpretable Latent Reasoning in Medical MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28422v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Latent reasoning enables reasoning over continuous hidden states rather than explicit tokens, avoiding the language bottleneck and inference overhead of chain-of-thought for medical VQA. | **[From Glance to Focus: Mitigating Recognition Laziness in MLLMs for Fine-Grained Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13154v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) generally fall short of expectations in Fine-Grained Visual Recognition (FGVR), although they exhibit promising FGVR foundations, i.e., visual perception and broad category knowledge. Different from these efforts, in this paper, we revisit the FGVR mechanism underlying MLLMs from both the empirical and theoretical perspectives and uncover an inference-time phenomenon termed FGVR Laziness. In brief, FGVR Laziness refers to the insufficient association between visual cues and category concepts under certain candidate category lists and recognition guidance, leaving the FGVR foundations of MLLMs largely under-utilized. To mitigate FGVR Laziness, we propose Laziness-resIstant Visual rEcognition (LIVE), a training-free framework which jointly refines candidate lists and recognition guidance to elicit the latent FGVR capability of MLLMs. Specifically, LIVE first performs query-adaptive candidate inference to derive a compact yet reliable candidate list. Meanwhile, LIVE constructs category-specific FGVR skills by mining the intra-category characteristics and inter-category differences. Finally, LIVE selectively invokes detailed skill guidance according to recognition uncertainty and category confusion, shifting MLLM-based FGVR from a cursory glance to a focus on associating discriminative visual cues with category concepts. Extensive experiments on 13 benchmarks against 24 state-of-the-art methods spanning fine-grained category recognition, action recognition, and general category recognition, verify the effectiveness and generalizability of LIVE across different MLLM backbones. | **[Scenes as an Attack Surface: Uncovering Scene-Grounded Safety Failures in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14094v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing jailbreak attacks have exposed serious safety weaknesses in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). | **[PixVL: Self-Supervised Training of Pixel-Level MLLMs via a Unified Mask--Text Consistency Cycle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01354v2)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent studies develop pixel-level multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that support both Region Segmentation and Region Understanding, extending multimodal interaction from whole images to specific objects and regions. | **[Can MLLMs Reason About Visual Persuasion? Evaluating the Efficacy and Faithfulness of Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08965v2)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persuasive visuals play a central role in advertising, public communication, and online media, making it increasingly important to understand whether an image is persuasive and why. | **[TaoD2C-Bench: Benchmarking MLLMs for Industrial UI Code Generation Beyond Visual Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10374v1)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) is moving beyond visual recognition to constraint-aware cross-modal reasoning. | **[DSV-Mem: Evaluating Multimodal Memory in Professional Workflows for MLLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08102v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational MLLM agents are increasingly expected to assist in professional workflows, from AI research and engineering design to product management and business operations. We also introduce a generation harness that produces evaluation suites by decoupling state-transition synthesis from conversation filling. Evaluation over 27 configurations spanning frontier and open-weight models and memory management methods reveals that the strongest baseline scores below 45% on DSV-Mem. This study investigates whether Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) exhibit human-like difficulty signatures in visual search tasks. Returning a few object tokens to the kept set recovers most of the lost accuracy. Selectors that avoid keeping redundant tokens stay close to the calibration of the unpruned model. We then use the confidence of the pruned model to set a per-question token budget. The model answers with few tokens first and again with all tokens when its confidence is low. Conformal risk control sets the threshold to bound the expected deviation from the unpruned model. | **[CoPre-MoE: Consolidating and Preserving MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18417v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal continual instruction tuning requires multimodal large language models to sequentially acquire new capabilities without catastrophic forgetting. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures offer a promising way to mitigate task interference through expert specialization and combination. However, while existing MoE-based methods avoid overwriting shared parameters by allocating separate experts for each task, this progressive expansion incurs severe cross-task redundancy and linear parameter growth. Specifically, experts across tasks often learn overlapping functions alongside task-specific capabilities. For instance, ScienceQA and TextVQA both locate relevant image regions. To curb parameter redundancy without inducing forgetting, we propose Consolidating and Preserving MoE (CoPre-MoE). For each incoming task, CoPre-MoE first learns task-specific MoE to capture specialized capabilities. To eliminate redundancy and reduce parameter growth across tasks, we design a shared-and-private expert architecture. Specifically, we first evaluate functional overlap to selectively consolidate overlapping experts across tasks into a fixed-capacity generic expert pool. We then factorize the remaining task-specific experts into shared projection bases and compact task-specific cores within a private expert pool. To mitigate forgetting, we construct the router by pairing each expert with learnable retrieval keys, where historical keys remain frozen during continual updates while newly added keys are regularized with an orthogonality penalty. At inference, input-dependent routing dynamically combines both pools without test-time task labels. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPre-MoE achieves superior performance while substantially reducing parameter overhead.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Public Participation in Local Government: An Audio-Based Instrument for Classifying Speakers in City Council Meetings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18072v1)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How much does the public actually speak in local government? Despite sustained interest in democratic participation, we lack large-scale measures of public voice at the local level, because the underlying record -- audio of open meetings -- has been prohibitively costly to convert into data. We contribute a measurement instrument: an audio-analysis pipeline that classifies each speaker in a city council meeting as an elected official or a member of the public directly from audio, and aggregates these labels into validated measures of participation such as floor-time share. The pipeline first segments each recording by speaker, then classifies each speaker with a multimodal large language model (MLLM), using the meeting's own temporal structure (neighboring speakers, iterative refinement, and a majority vote across three classifiers) as supporting components. We collect 1,618 recordings from seven U.S. cities (2017-2023); after diarization and quality control, the analytic corpus is 1,565 meetings from five cities. The task is non-trivial: audio quality is uneven, members of the public speak informally and are often introduced only briefly, and speech frequently overlaps. Even so, segmentation reaches 89% accuracy (mean pairwise kappa=0.84), and the classifier reaches 87.7% accuracy (kappa=0.736, public-participant F1=0.832) on ground truth from five cities (N=1,761 speakers) -- agreement comparable to that among the human annotators themselves. At scale, members of the public receive a median of just 10% of floor time, the top speaker is an official in over 94% of meetings, and public participation declined over COVID-19 without recovering by 2023. | **[The Alignment Illusion in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30210v2)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Layer-wise visual-text similarity in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is widely interpreted as evidence that the language model progressively integrates visual content into a shared representation space. Across 13 MLLMs from five families spanning 0.5B to 72B parameters, replacing projector-output visual tokens with Gaussian noise sharply reduces task accuracy, yet four standard scalar measures (CKA, SVCCA, MIR, and the leading principal-angle cosine) fail to consistently separate the corrupted stream from the original. We introduce the principal-angle gap (PA gap), defined as the difference between the top two principal-angle cosines, to distinguish leading-direction dominance from the broader overlap observed with structured visual content. Under graded visual corruption, the PA gap tracks task accuracy more consistently than the scalar scores we consider; under a structured but irrelevant image, it further exposes regimes in which internal geometry and task accuracy come apart. | **[Speculative Decoding Reimagined for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14260v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) deliver strong performance across vision-language tasks but suffer from high autoregressive decoding latency. MSD is motivated by two key observations: text and visual tokens should be handled separately during drafting, and an effective draft model requires both strong language modeling and visual perception capabilities. Accordingly, MSD decouples text and visual token processing in the draft model and applies token-specific strategies to improve drafting efficiency. We further introduce a two-stage training strategy. The draft model is first trained on text-only instruction-tuning data to strengthen its language modeling ability and is then progressively exposed to multimodal data to develop visual perception while preserving its language competence. Extensive experiments across multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that MSD achieves up to $2.29\times$ inference speedup for LLaVA-1.5-7B and $2.46\times$ for LLaVA-1.5-13B without sacrificing generation quality. These results establish MSD as an effective and general approach for accelerating multimodal autoregressive generation. | **[Layer Roles Matter: Mitigating Modality Imbalance in Multimodal Emotion Reasoning via Adaptive Layer Profiling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11907v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Emotion Reasoning (MER) requires perceiving and integrating emotion cues from multiple modalities. In this paper, we conduct an in-depth analysis on MER models and uncover two key empirical insights. (i) In MER, modality imbalance manifests two distinct failure modes: unimodal over-reliance and insufficient cross-modal utilization, which call for different intervention strategies. (ii) MLLM layers exhibit implicit functional hierarchies in multimodal emotion processing, playing separable roles in stabilizing unimodal representations and integrating cross-modal information. Motivated by these observations, we propose a Layer-Aware Modality Rebalancing (LAMR) framework by explicitly aligning intervention strategies with layer roles. Specifically, we first introduce two quantized metrics for adaptive layer role profiling. In each layer, Modality Entropy (ME) measures the dispersion of modality contributions, and Cross-modal Intensity (CMI) captures the strength of cross-modal interactions. Then, we respectively enhance modality equilibrium in stabilization layers and cross-modal propagation in integration layers. Extensive experiments on two comprehensive MER benchmarks demonstrate that LAMR effectively mitigates modality imbalance and improves emotion reasoning across diverse MLLM backbones, without additional training.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedMMV: A Controllable Multimodal Multi-Agent Framework for Reliable and Verifiable Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24314v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) suffer from unstable reasoning due to the lack of mechanisms for controlling reasoning trajectories, often leading to hallucinated conclusions. | **[Linear Model Merging Unlocks Simple and Scalable Multimodal Data Mixture Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04937v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Selecting the best data mixture is critical for successful Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) of Multimodal Large Language Models. In this work, we take the best of both worlds, studying model merging as an efficient strategy for estimating the performance of different data mixtures. We train domain-specific multimodal experts and evaluate their weighted parameter-space combinations to estimate the efficacy of corresponding data mixtures. We conduct extensive experiments on 14 multimodal benchmarks, and empirically demonstrate that the merged proxy models consistently select near-optimal mixtures. This decouples the search for optimal mixtures from the resource-intensive training process, thereby providing a scalable and efficient strategy for navigating the complex landscape of mixture weights. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/BerasiDavide/mLLMs_merging_4_DMO.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR), 2026</p></details> |
| **[Review the Episodes, Refine the Lessons: Simple Memory Evolution for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11509v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent studies of embodied agents based on frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) develop harness engineering around the MLLM, such as harnesses with post-trained policies and recursive self-improvement loops. | **[How to "train" a surgeon? | **[Multimodal Graph Learning: A Survey of Models, Benchmarks, and Frontiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12990v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal attributed graphs (MAGs), in which nodes carry text, images, and other modalities while edges encode relations among entities, underlie e-commerce, social media, content platforms, and creative applications. Learning on MAGs has developed along two complementary lines: graph-centric methods redesign how modalities are fused, propagated, and connected within message passing, while foundation-model-centric methods pretrain transferable multimodal graph encoders or bring graph context into multimodal large language models for prediction and generation. In parallel, several benchmarks have appeared with different datasets and evaluation protocols. This survey takes an algorithm-centric and evaluation-grounded view of the field and makes three contributions. Modeling: we introduce a unified message-passing formulation and organize methods into five integration levels (feature, propagation, topology, learning, and foundation model), covering discriminative and graph-conditioned generative models; within this formulation we derive representative methods as special cases, compare their assumptions, expressiveness, and cost, and identify cross-modal sensitivity as a property that separates otherwise similar architectures. Datasets and evaluation: we review recent benchmarks, summarize their datasets and metrics, define model-independent descriptors (label homophily, average degree, clustering, and edge provenance), and map each evaluation protocol to the baselines it uses. Frontiers: we connect multimodal graph learning to AI agents, covering the multimodal graphs that agents perceive and remember and graph engineering, the practice of organizing agent workflows as explicit graphs; we then discuss open problems, concrete efforts for the community, and research directions, including when graph structure helps in the presence of strong multimodal foundation models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evidence-Grounded Iterative Reasoning for the Diagnosis of Diffuse Cystic Lung Diseases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12981v1)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Overlapping imaging manifestations complicate the differential diagnosis of Diffuse Cystic Lung Diseases (DCLDs), while limited annotated data constrain supervised model development. Competing hypotheses guide axial CT inspection to identify local findings that, together with clinical context, inform retrieval from a disease-specific knowledge graph. Accumulated patient observations and retrieved knowledge are jointly assessed for diagnostic support and refutation to revise the diagnostic state. Updated hypotheses and unresolved distinctions guide subsequent inspection, forming a feedback loop between evidence acquisition and hypothesis verification. Multicenter evaluation demonstrates that DCLDs-EIR consistently improves diagnostic accuracy over baselines and enables traceable differential diagnosis through explicit links between diagnostic hypotheses, patient observations, and disease-specific knowledge.</p></details> |  |
| **[Conceal, Reconstruct, Jailbreak: Exploiting the Reconstruction-Concealment Tradeoff in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05709v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Intent-obfuscation-based jailbreak attacks on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) transform a harmful query into a concealed multimodal input to bypass safety mechanisms. We study these attacks through the lens of a reconstruction-concealment tradeoff: the transformed input must sufficiently obscure harmful intent while preserving enough information for the victim model to recover the underlying request. Through a reconstruction and concealment analysis of representative black-box methods, we find that existing transformations struggle to balance this tradeoff, limiting their effectiveness. In contrast, we show that character-removed variants, constructed by randomly removing characters from the original query, achieve a better balance, motivating their use as the basis for our attack construction. Building on this observation, we propose concealment-aware variant construction, which selects character-removed variants with low harmful keyword/phrase alignment and high mutual diversity, and instantiates them through five prompting strategies that distribute the transformed information across text and image channels. We further introduce keyword/phrase-related distractor images that depict the harmful concept in diverse contexts, providing more effective distraction than generic distractor images. | **[Beyond RGB: Benchmarking and Enhancing MLLMs for Hyperspectral Image Understanding via Training-Free Reasoning Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08884v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on RGB image understanding, yet their ability to use spectral evidence beyond the visible range remains largely unexplored. | **[Scaling Laws for Multimodal Data Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14423v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier AI systems are increasingly natively multimodal, jointly pretrained on multiple modalities, such as text, vision, and audio. In this work, we derive data mixing scaling laws for three modalities: text, vision, and audio. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) increasingly adopt Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, as MoEs scale efficiently and partially limit cross-modal interference. We therefore use MoEs to conduct 268 experiments ranging from 457M to 8.3B parameters, on up to 150 billion tokens. Our work leads to multiple actionable insights into multimodal data mixing for training MLLMs. First, compute optima are modality-specific: audio modality is model-heavy, favoring scaling parameters over tokens, while text and vision require balanced scaling of model size and data. Second, repeating vision or audio data beyond 2x yields negligible benefits. Finally, cross-modal interactions are highly asymmetric: vision strongly interferes with audio, whereas audio's interference with learning text and vision is negligible. Overall, we provide a principled foundation for understanding the multimodal data mixtures needed to train frontier MLLMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a spotlight in NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

| **[ClinFusion: A Vision-Centric Multimodal LLM System for Holistic Medical Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24743v4)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) hold immense potential to revolutionize clinical practice, yet deploying them in the medical domain is fundamentally a vision-centric challenge: models must absorb knowledge from heterogeneous 2D and 3D medical images, and evaluation protocols must align with radiologists' clinical practice and provide an accurate, fine-grained and factualness-driven assessment. We show that ClinFusion sets a new state-of-the-art across a comprehensive suite of 2D and 3D multimodal medical benchmarks---spanning visual question answering, report generation, and instruction following---as well as textual medical tasks, outperforming leading open-source medical MLLMs (\textit{e.g.}, Hulu-Med, Lingshu) on 20 out of 24 benchmarks and demonstrating multimodal capabilities better than powerful proprietary models such as GPT-5.2 and Gemini-3-Flash on 13 out of 16 benchmarks, and can be further augmented with agentic tool use for retrieval-augmented and tool-assisted clinical workflows. | **[One Agent to Guide Them All: Empowering MLLMs for Vision-and-Language Navigation via Explicit World Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15400v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A navigation agent must understand high-level semantic instructions and reason about precise spatial structure. | **[Reason First, Retrieve What Matters: Hypothesis-Guided Keyframe Selection for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10609v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long video understanding requires models to identify and reason over sparse, temporally distributed evidence within limited frame budgets. Existing keyframe selection methods typically retrieve query-relevant frames from a densely sampled candidate set before inference. However, global image-text matching incurs unnecessary computation when a small set of uniformly sampled frames already provides sufficient evidence. HypoKFS first reasons over sparsely sampled frames to construct an initial hypothesis space. The resulting answer distribution determines whether additional frame selection is needed. When needed, the retained hypotheses guide the selection of discriminative frames, followed by renewed inference and hypothesis refinement. This iterative process directs additional visual computation toward unresolved hypotheses while avoiding unnecessary global frame matching. Experiments on Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and MLVU demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple MLLM backbones. | **[MSM-Mem: A Universal Medical Structured Multimodal Memory Framework for Medical AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21810v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical decision-making is inherently experience-driven: physicians progressively refine their reasoning by synthesizing patient history, multimodal observations, and prior diagnostic experiences across interactions. In contrast, current multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based medical AI agents largely operate as stateless inference systems, generating decisions independently for each interaction without retaining or internalizing experiential knowledge. This discrepancy limits their ability to progressively improve reasoning reliability through usage and adapt to longitudinal patient contexts in real-world clinical workflows. In this study, we propose Medical Structured Multimodal Memory (MSM-Mem), an agentic memory framework that enables medical AI agents to evolve through accumulated clinical experiences. MSM-Mem organizes heterogeneous clinical experiences into semantic, episodic, and visual memory and incrementally updates them during inference, allowing the agent to retrieve prior experiences to inform current reasoning and progressively refine decision-making over time. | **[Mental Imagery That Matters: Curing Latent Collapse in Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10423v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mental simulation, the ability to reason by constructing and updating internal visual states, is central to flexible human cognition, but remains elusive in current multimodal large language models. In this paper, we ask whether these latent visual states actually matter. Through a direct perturbation analysis across representative latent-reasoning MLLMs, we find that they often do not: dropping, replacing, or shuffling generated visual latents has only minor effects on the model's own final-answer probability, while equivalent perturbations to the linguistic reasoning trace are substantially more damaging. We call this failure mode Mental Simulation Collapse (MSC): the model appears to perform interleaved visual reasoning, but routes most answer-relevant computation through text. We further diagnose that MSC follows naturally from existing training objectives: visual alignment can make generated latents resemble helper-image embeddings, but it does not require the decoder to rely on them; subsequent text-only supervision further leaves the semantic role of the latent pathway unidentifiable. To address this, we introduce AnchorSim (Anchored Mental Simulation), a contrastive distillation framework that trains models to make their generated visual latents consequential. These results show that quantitative transcriptomic measurements and biological language can be integrated within a general conversational model, enabling both predictive tasks and language-guided interpretation of experimental samples.</p></details> | <details><summary>47 pa...</summary><p>47 pages (10 pages main text, 29 pages appendix), 1 figure, 16 tables</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Graph RAG for Long-range Visually Rich Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28780v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are widely applied to visual document understanding. However, comprehending long documents remains an issue by the limited context window. Though recent multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (MMRAG) can address this challenge by retrieving relevant pages. It still struggles with the visual question answering (VQA) requiring holistic comprehension of a document. To cope with this, knowledge graph (KG) that summarizes global knowledge of a document can provide an effective solution. However, most existing LLM-based KG construction methods handle only the language modality, leaving the automatic creation of multimodal KGs (MMKGs) for visually rich documents largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce a multimodal graph-based RAG approach to tackle this problem. Existing LLM-based KG methods evaluate the QA performance relying on indirect evidence such as comprehensiveness, diversity, empowerment, and so on. The lack of annotated datasets for comprehensive document-level VQA poses a significant challenge to effective model evaluation. To overcome this limitation, we also introduce a new benchmark, DLVQA (document-level VQA), which provides reference summaries and corresponding supporting facts for global document-level questions. Experimental results show that our approach outperforms existing MMRAG or KG-based approaches on multi-hop QA/VQA benchmarks and DLVQA.</p></details> | WACV 2027 |
| **[Unified Hallucination Fuzzing for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07525v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hallucination remains a persistent challenge for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), severely limiting their reliability in high-stakes applications. First, we introduce UniHall, a fine-grained dataset grounded in a unified taxonomy spanning Object, Instruction, and Knowledge dimensions. Second, to address benchmark saturation, we propose Self-Adaptive Multimodal Fuzzing (SAMF), a self-adaptive framework that employs evolutionary mutation strategies to explore the boundaries of model hallucinations. Crucially, to ensure reliable assessment of dynamic inputs, SAMF incorporates a structured metric suite driven by an ensemble of multi-modal oracles. Our extensive experiments reveal that state-of-the-art MLLMs exhibit significant performance degradation under fuzzing compared to conventional settings, exposing a dissociation between reasoning capabilities and factual grounding. Furthermore, we identify a helpfulness-hallucination trade-off, where reinforcement learning alignment inadvertently exacerbates sycophancy in instruction-following tasks. The framework, code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/LanceZPF/EvalHall.</p></details> | 47 pages, 17 figures |
| **[OmniOPSD: Rationale-Privileged On-Policy Self-Distillation for Affective Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15920v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have advanced affective computing by enabling nuanced reasoning over visual, acoustic, and linguistic evidence. A key approach to improving their generalization is outcome-based reinforcement learning, which converts existing label-level annotations into rewards for final-answer correctness. To address this challenge, we introduce OmniOPSD, the first on-policy self-distillation framework for omni-modal affective reasoning. At its core, OmniOPSD transforms label-level supervision into evidence-rich, teacher-only rationales by leveraging reference affective judgments as semantic priors for offline multimodal evidence elicitation from frontier MLLMs. Through rationale-privileged self-distillation, it couples on-policy student rollouts with dense, evidence-informed token-level supervision from a local self-teacher, allowing the student to learn along its own trajectories rather than imitate fixed reasoning traces. This decoupling of offline evidence acquisition from local policy learning enables training without additional human annotation, frontier-model logits, or online frontier-model calls, while preserving student-only inference from the original task input. At both 3B and 7B scales, OmniOPSD outperforms supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and group relative policy optimization (GRPO) in mean macro-F1 on both in-domain and out-of-domain evaluations. | **[Beyond Visual Boundaries: Rethinking Scene Segmentation for Movie RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28699v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding long-form video remains a fundamental challenge for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Scene segmentation, a long-studied problem that partitions movies into semantically coherent units, is a natural candidate for defining such retrieval units. We reexamine whether existing methods actually serve this role through comprehensive evaluation on downstream movie understanding tasks, and find that they do not provide a consistent or dominant advantage over naive uniform temporal chunking. Our audit of the most standard scene segmentation benchmarks reveals why: current annotations prioritize visually salient transitions over narrative event structure. Motivated by this mismatch, we introduce NarraScene, a narrative-centric scene segmentation dataset annotated with a three-level cognitive taxonomy spanning physical, character, and narrative change, where every valid boundary requires a narrative-level shift. When used as retrieval units, these narrative-grounded segments outperform uniform chunking on downstream movie understanding tasks, suggesting that the central challenge for scene segmentation in movie RAG is not detecting boundaries, but identifying the narrative event units that matter for movie understanding. | **[Visual-Redundancy-Controlled Parallel Decoding for Diffusion-Based Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25820v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion-based multimodal large language models (dMLLMs) decode by iteratively predicting tokens at multiple masked positions in parallel. We identify a step-level limitation of this strategy in multimodal settings: high-confidence tokens selected in the same step can rely on overlapping visual grounding, introducing visual redundancy among the committed tokens and leaving less complementary visual grounding available for later decoding. To quantify this effect, we introduce the Visual Redundancy Index (VRI), which measures visual grounding overlap among tokens committed in parallel. To control this redundancy during decoding, we propose Visual-Redundancy-Controlled Decoding (VRCD), a training-free inference-time decoding method that uses token-to-image attention to prioritize visually complementary positions. Across diverse multimodal benchmarks, VRCD reduces visual redundancy and remaining-position entropy with modest runtime overhead. In longer decoding experiments, it also achieves relative accuracy gains of up to 18.8% on M^3CoT and 6.9% on MMBench over confidence-based decoding. These chains are then distilled into a student model that reasons only from real observations. Code: https://github.com/Alibaba-VELLDEPTH/DreamRealm . Model: https://huggingface.co/Alibaba-VELLDEPTH/Qwen35-9b-DreamRealm</p></details> |

To address these limitations, we propose PSGAgent, an agentic framework that enhances visual perception for PSG through a think-act-observe loop. | **[BEAKER: An Expert-Curated Benchmark for Embodied Brains in Self-Driving Chemical Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06911v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-Driving Chemical Laboratories (SDCLs) are moving chemical experimentation toward embodied automation, where machines must perceive laboratory scenes, reason about experimental states, and act under physical, procedural, and safety constraints. | **[Where to Connect? Boosting MLLMs via Dynamic Gated Pathways across ALL ViT and LLM Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06844v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central yet under-explored question in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is where to connect---how visual information from a hierarchical vision encoder should be wired into the layer-wise semantics of a large language model. Accepted at the 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |

| **[Benchmarking Fine-Grained Visual Reasoning for Remote Sensing in Urban Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06770v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-grained visual reasoning is essential for remote sensing in urban management, yet remains underexplored in the evaluation of multimodal large language models (MLLMs).
